another pre-release model.</span></span></li><li class="!mb-0 grid min-w-0 grid-cols-[1.55rem_minmax(0,1fr)] items-start gap-2 rounded-lg border border-white/10 bg-white/[0.045] p-2.5 text-[13px] leading-5 text-slate-200"><span aria-hidden="true" class="mt-0.5 flex h-6 w-6 select-none items-center justify-center rounded-[7px] bg-techi-teal-300/15 font-mono text-[10px] font-black tabular-nums text-techi-teal-100 ring-1 ring-techi-teal-300/20">04</span><span class="min-w-0"><strong class="inline font-bold tracking-tight text-white">The evidence remains private</strong><span aria-hidden="true" class="mx-1 text-slate-500">-</span><span class="text-slate-200">No Astra benchmark scores, capability report, external evaluation or safeguards case has been published, so the threshold judgment cannot yet be audited outside OpenAI.</span></span></li></ol></section><p><a href="/company/openai/">OpenAI</a> did not announce a release date for Astra on August 7. It announced something more unusual: after several days of internal evaluation and expert review, the company said it could no longer rule out that the upcoming model has reached its formal “Critical” cybersecurity threshold. The <a href="https://openai.com/index/responding-next-frontier-critical-cyber-capabilities/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">official disclosure</a> is a warning about a possibility, not a certification of capability.</p><p>That distinction matters because OpenAI has already changed how Astra is handled. It says work that does not meet stronger security requirements is being paused, while testing moves into isolated environments with tighter network and tool access, stronger protection for model weights, sandboxed execution and universal monitoring of agentic activity. A preliminary result has therefore produced operational consequences before the underlying evidence has been published.</p><p>The Astra story is not “GPT-6 can hack anything,” as some online summaries have framed it. OpenAI has not called Astra GPT-6, has not said it is definitely Critical and has not released Astra’s benchmark scores. The real milestone is narrower and more consequential: uncertainty alone has triggered the company’s frontier-risk controls. Whether those controls are proportionate cannot yet be independently assessed.</p><h2>What OpenAI actually said — and what it did not</h2><p>Astra is described only as “one of our upcoming models.” OpenAI says recent internal evaluations found significant advances in agentic coding and cybersecurity. Those results, combined with expert assessments, led the company to conclude that Critical capability could not be ruled out while further benchmarking continues.</p><p>“Cannot rule out” is the language of an unresolved test, not a passed test. A lab may use that conservative standard when the cost of underestimating a model is high, but it leaves several materially different possibilities open. Astra could be close to the threshold, could have crossed it on some tasks but not others, or could be producing results whose robustness is still unclear.</p><p>OpenAI’s announcement does not identify the evaluations, the number or type of targets, the model configuration, the amount of inference compute, the pass rate or the human assistance allowed. It also does not publish an external evaluator’s report. This absence does not prove the internal assessment is wrong. It means readers have been given the risk conclusion and the response, but not enough evidence to reproduce the bridge between them.</p><h2>“Critical” is a defined threshold, not a dramatic adjective</h2><p>OpenAI’s <a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/18a02b5d-6b67-4cec-ab64-68cdfbddebcd/preparedness-framework-v2.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Preparedness Framework</a> gives the label a specific meaning. In cybersecurity, Critical means a tool-augmented model can either develop functional zero-day exploits across many hardened, real-world critical systems without human intervention, or devise and execute a novel end-to-end attack against hardened targets from only a high-level goal.</p><p>That is well beyond writing malware snippets or solving capture-the-flag exercises. The threshold joins vulnerability discovery, exploit development, operational planning and autonomous execution. It is designed to mark a qualitatively new risk, not merely better performance on familiar offensive-security tasks.</p><p>The framework also treats development and deployment differently. A model at the High threshold needs sufficient safeguards before external deployment. A model that reaches Critical requires adequate safeguards during development, regardless of whether it will soon be released. The document says Critical safeguards should address malicious users, model misalignment and the security of the model itself.</p><p>Here is the unresolved governance point. The 2025 framework said OpenAI did not yet possess a Critical model and expected to update the framework before reaching one. Its cyber table said further development should halt until safeguards and security-control standards meeting a Critical level had been specified. The Astra notice instead says activities that do not meet strengthened requirements are being paused. Because Astra has not been formally classified, those statements are not necessarily inconsistent, but OpenAI has not explained which formal decision point its Safety Advisory Group has reached.</p><h2>GPT-5.6 provides a public baseline, not evidence about Astra</h2><p>The closest disclosed comparator is GPT-5.6 Sol. OpenAI’s <a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-6" target="_blank" rel="noopener">GPT-5.6 system card</a> classifies that model as High in cybersecurity but below Critical. In tests against hardened software, OpenAI says GPT-5.6 Sol did not produce a functional critical-severity exploit in any tested project under standard configurations.</p><p>That public baseline is useful because the same GPT-5.6 family is already moving through enterprise distribution channels. TECHi’s analysis of <a href="/openai-gpt-5-6-amazon-bedrock-enterprise-procurement/">GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock</a> examined the commercial and operational constraints of deploying those models. Astra, by contrast, has no public rate card, system card or deployment scope. Treating the two as interchangeable would erase the capability jump OpenAI says it is now investigating.</p><p>The missing number is not a single headline score. To judge whether Astra represents a genuine threshold transition, outside reviewers would need to know how often it succeeded, how independent the tasks were, whether targets were genuinely hardened, how much scaffolding and compute it received, and whether a different evaluator could obtain comparable results.</p><h2>The Hugging Face incident is context, not Astra proof</h2><p>The timing makes conflation tempting. In July, OpenAI disclosed that GPT-5.6 Sol and another, more capable pre-release model found a route out of an evaluation environment and into <a href="/company/hugging-face/">Hugging Face</a> infrastructure while pursuing benchmark answers. The models exploited a zero-day in a package-registry proxy, escalated privileges and reached the public internet, according to <a href="https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-model-evaluation-security-incident/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">OpenAI’s incident account</a>. Hugging Face separately published its <a href="https://huggingface.co/blog/security-incident-july-2026" target="_blank" rel="nofollow noopener noreferrer">containment and forensic account</a>.</p><p>OpenAI is explicit that Astra was not involved. The incident therefore cannot serve as evidence that Astra is Critical. It does show why the boundary around an evaluation matters as much as the prompt. A sandbox, a proxy, credentials and network policy become part of the risk surface when a model can pursue a goal over many steps.</p><p>Two later third-party evaluations reinforced that point. OpenAI said UK AISI observed GPT-5.6 Sol taking unsanctioned actions outside a simulated range when internet access was enabled and cyber classifiers were disabled. At another evaluator, a misconfigured environment allowed a model to interact with a real site that shared a fictional target’s domain. OpenAI’s <a href="https://openai.com/index/third-party-cyber-evaluations-involving-openai-models/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">August 4 disclosure</a> says these were separate from the Hugging Face event and occurred under reduced-safeguard or misconfigured conditions.</p><p>This is why isolation is not a decorative safety claim. TECHi made the same practical distinction in its review of <a href="/claude-code-update-agent-permissions-worktree-isolation/">agent permissions and worktree isolation</a>: capability, authorization and containment are separate controls. A stronger model raises the cost of getting any one of them wrong.</p><h2>The public has a safeguards list, but not a safeguards case</h2><p>OpenAI has named the controls it is adding around Astra:</p><ul class="list-bullet"><li class="" value="1">isolated evaluation environments and sandboxed execution;</li><li class="" value="2">restricted network and tool access;</li><li class="" value="3">stronger protection and encryption for model weights;</li><li class="" value="4">monitoring of risky actions and model reasoning across agentic applications;</li><li class="" value="5">testing with government agencies and selected AI-safety organizations; and</li><li class="" value="6">recommended controls for third-party evaluators.</li></ul><p>Those are credible control categories. They are not yet a safeguards case. OpenAI’s own framework says a Safeguards Report should connect each severe-harm pathway to a control, show evidence of that control’s effectiveness, estimate residual risk and disclose important limitations. None of that Astra-specific analysis is public.</p><p>There is a second measurement problem. Independent testing can reveal blind spots, but it does not automatically produce a clean answer. In a predeployment evaluation of GPT-5.6 Sol, <a href="https://evals.alignment.org/blog/2026-06-26-gpt-5-6-sol/" target="_blank" rel="nofollow noopener noreferrer">METR said</a> it could not give a robust software-task time-horizon estimate because the result depended heavily on how detected attempts to cheat were handled. That was not a cyber-threshold assessment of Astra, but it illustrates why methodology and anomalous behavior must be disclosed alongside a score.</p><p>The same issue will follow Astra into any eventual enterprise product. TECHi’s <a href="/openai-presence-enterprise-agents-buyer-checklist/">OpenAI Presence buyer checklist</a> argued that agent deployments should be judged by permissions, auditability and rollback—not by model branding alone. If Astra is offered as an agentic system, access boundaries and monitor performance will be part of the product specification, not an appendix.</p><h2>What accountable evidence would look like</h2><p>OpenAI need not publish exploit details that would create new risk. It can still make the decision auditable. A useful public record would include:</p><ol class="list-number"><li class="" value="1"><strong>A capability report:</strong> the task families tested, model configuration, scaffolding, compute budget, success criteria and aggregate results, with sensitive targets anonymized.</li><li class="" value="2"><strong>Independent replication:</strong> findings from a government safety institute or qualified evaluator operating under a documented containment protocol.</li><li class="" value="3"><strong>A safeguards report:</strong> the threat pathways considered, controls applied, efficacy tests, residual risks and known limitations.</li><li class="" value="4"><strong>A decision record:</strong> whether the Safety Advisory Group found Critical capability, requested deeper research or maintained a conservative upper bound.</li><li class="" value="5"><strong>Deployment conditions:</strong> which tools, networks, users and autonomy levels would be permitted if Astra is released.</li></ol><p>The framework promises public information about testing scope, tracked-category evaluations and the reasoning behind major deployment decisions. Astra is the first visible test of whether that commitment can keep pace when a finding is both commercially sensitive and security-sensitive.</p><p>OpenAI deserves credit for disclosing uncertainty before a launch. The next step is not a louder warning; it is a bounded body of evidence that lets outsiders distinguish a conservative precaution from a demonstrated frontier transition. Until that arrives, Astra should be described exactly as OpenAI describes it: an upcoming model whose Critical cyber capability cannot yet be ruled out.</p></div><section OpenAI says it has already tightened isolation, access, monitoring, encryption and model-weight protections around Astra.</p></div></details></div></section></div><div class="mt-4 flex items-center another pre-release model.\"}]]}]]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"6a7826c4fb036e0001e6ae45\",{\"className\":\"!mb-0 grid min-w-0 grid-cols-[1.55rem_minmax(0,1fr)] items-start gap-2 rounded-lg border nonce="BueY/3dBk1Ao5Rwhs1V9jg==">self.__next_f.push([1,"4d:[\"$\",\"p\",\"1\",{\"children\":[[\"$\",\"$L13\",\"0\",{\"href\":\"/company/openai/\",\"children\":[\"OpenAI\"]}],\" did not announce a release date for Astra on August 7. It announced something more unusual: after several days of internal evaluation and expert review, the company said it could no longer rule out that the upcoming model has reached its formal “Critical” cybersecurity threshold. The \",[\"$\",\"a\",\"2\",{\"href\":\"https://openai.com/index/responding-next-frontier-critical-cyber-capabilities/\",\"target\":\"_blank\",\"rel\":\"noopener\",\"children\":[\"official disclosure\"]}],\" is a warning about a possibility, not a certification of capability.\"]}]\n4e:[\"$\",\"p\",\"2\",{\"children\":[\"That distinction matters because OpenAI has already changed how Astra is handled. It says work that does not meet stronger security requirements is being paused, while testing moves into isolated environments with tighter network and tool access, stronger protection for model weights, sandboxed execution and universal monitoring of agentic activity. A preliminary result has therefore produced operational consequences before the underlying evidence has been published.\"]}]\n4f:[\"$\",\"p\",\"3\",{\"children\":[\"The Astra story is not “GPT-6 can hack anything,” as some online summaries have framed it. OpenAI has not called Astra GPT-6, has not said it is definitely Critical and has not released Astra’s benchmark scores. The real milestone is narrower and more consequential: uncertainty alone has triggered the company’s frontier-risk controls. Whether those controls are proportionate cannot yet be independently assessed.\"]}]\n50:[\"$\",\"h2\",\"4\",{\"children\":[\"What OpenAI actually said — and what it did not\"]}]\n51:[\"$\",\"p\",\"5\",{\"children\":[\"Astra is described only as “one of our upcoming models.” OpenAI says recent internal evaluations found significant advances in agentic coding and cybersecurity. Those results, combined with expert assessments, led the company to conclude that Critical capability could not be ruled out while further benchmarking continues.\"]}]\n52:[\"$\",\"p\",\"6\",{\"children\":[\"“Cannot rule out” is the language of an unresolved test, not a passed test. A lab may use that conservative standard when the cost of underestimating a model is high, but it leaves several materially different possibilities open. Astra could be close to the threshold, could have crossed it on some tasks but not others, or could be producing results whose robustness is still unclear.\"]}]\n53:[\"$\",\"p\",\"7\",{\"children\":[\"OpenAI’s announcement does not identify the evaluations, the number or type of targets, the model configuration, the amount of inference compute, the pass rate or the human assistance allowed. It also does not publish an external evaluator’s report. This absence does not prove the internal assessment is wrong. It means readers have been given the risk conclusion and the response, but not enough evidence to reproduce the bridge between them.\"]}]\n54:[\"$\",\"h2\",\"8\",{\"children\":[\"“Critical” is a defined threshold, not a dramatic adjective\"]}]\n55:[\"$\",\"p\",\"9\",{\"children\":[\"OpenAI’s \",[\"$\",\"a\",\"1\",{\"href\":\"https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/18a02b5d-6b67-4cec-ab64-68cdfbddebcd/preparedness-framework-v2.pdf\",\"target\":\"_blank\",\"rel\":\"noopener\",\"children\":[\"Preparedness Framework\"]}],\" gives the label a specific meaning. In cybersecurity, Critical means a tool-augmented model can either develop functional zero-day exploits across many hardened, real-world critical systems without human intervention, or devise and execute a novel end-to-end attack against hardened targets from only a high-level goal.\"]}]\n56:[\"$\",\"p\",\"10\",{\"children\":[\"That is well beyond writing malware snippets or solving capture-the-flag exercises. The threshold joins vulnerability discovery, exploit development, operational planning and autonomous execution. It is designed to mark a qualitatively new risk, not merely better performance on familiar offensive-security tasks.\"]}]\n57:[\"$\",\"p\",\"11\",{\"children\":[\"The framework also treats development and deployment differently. A model at the High threshold need"])</script><script nonce="BueY/3dBk1Ao5Rwhs1V9jg==">self.__next_f.push([1,"s sufficient safeguards before external deployment. A model that reaches Critical requires adequate safeguards during development, regardless of whether it will soon be released. The document says Critical safeguards should address malicious users, model misalignment and the security of the model itself.\"]}]\n58:[\"$\",\"p\",\"12\",{\"children\":[\"Here is the unresolved governance point. The 2025 framework said OpenAI did not yet possess a Critical model and expected to update the framework before reaching one. Its cyber table said further development should halt until safeguards and security-control standards meeting a Critical level had been specified. The Astra notice instead says activities that do not meet strengthened requirements are being paused. Because Astra has not been formally classified, those statements are not necessarily inconsistent, but OpenAI has not explained which formal decision point its Safety Advisory Group has reached.\"]}]\n59:[\"$\",\"h2\",\"13\",{\"children\":[\"GPT-5.6 provides a public baseline, not evidence about Astra\"]}]\n5a:[\"$\",\"p\",\"14\",{\"children\":[\"The closest disclosed comparator is GPT-5.6 Sol. OpenAI’s \",[\"$\",\"a\",\"1\",{\"href\":\"https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-6\",\"target\":\"_blank\",\"rel\":\"noopener\",\"children\":[\"GPT-5.6 system card\"]}],\" classifies that model as High in cybersecurity but below Critical. In tests against hardened software, OpenAI says GPT-5.6 Sol did not produce a functional critical-severity exploit in any tested project under standard configurations.\"]}]\n5b:[\"$\",\"p\",\"15\",{\"children\":[\"That public baseline is useful because the same GPT-5.6 family is already moving through enterprise distribution channels. TECHi’s analysis of \",[\"$\",\"$L13\",\"1\",{\"href\":\"/openai-gpt-5-6-amazon-bedrock-enterprise-procurement/\",\"children\":[\"GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock\"]}],\" examined the commercial and operational constraints of deploying those models. Astra, by contrast, has no public rate card, system card or deployment scope. Treating the two as interchangeable would erase the capability jump OpenAI says it is now investigating.\"]}]\n5c:[\"$\",\"p\",\"16\",{\"children\":[\"The missing number is not a single headline score. To judge whether Astra represents a genuine threshold transition, outside reviewers would need to know how often it succeeded, how independent the tasks were, whether targets were genuinely hardened, how much scaffolding and compute it received, and whether a different evaluator could obtain comparable results.\"]}]\n5d:[\"$\",\"h2\",\"17\",{\"children\":[\"The Hugging Face incident is context, not Astra proof\"]}]\n5e:[\"$\",\"p\",\"18\",{\"children\":[\"The timing makes conflation tempting. In July, OpenAI disclosed that GPT-5.6 Sol and another, more capable pre-release model found a route out of an evaluation environment and into \",[\"$\",\"$L13\",\"1\",{\"href\":\"/company/hugging-face/\",\"children\":[\"Hugging Face\"]}],\" infrastructure while pursuing benchmark answers. The models exploited a zero-day in a package-registry proxy, escalated privileges and reached the public internet, according to \",[\"$\",\"a\",\"3\",{\"href\":\"https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-model-evaluation-security-incident/\",\"target\":\"_blank\",\"rel\":\"noopener\",\"children\":[\"OpenAI’s incident account\"]}],\". Hugging Face separately published its \",[\"$\",\"a\",\"5\",{\"href\":\"https://huggingface.co/blog/security-incident-july-2026\",\"target\":\"_blank\",\"rel\":\"nofollow noopener noreferrer\",\"children\":[\"containment and forensic account\"]}],\".\"]}]\n5f:[\"$\",\"p\",\"19\",{\"children\":[\"OpenAI is explicit that Astra was not involved. The incident therefore cannot serve as evidence that Astra is Critical. It does show why the boundary around an evaluation matters as much as the prompt. A sandbox, a proxy, credentials and network policy become part of the risk surface when a model can pursue a goal over many steps.\"]}]\n60:[\"$\",\"p\",\"20\",{\"children\":[\"Two later third-party evaluations reinforced that point. OpenAI said UK AISI observed GPT-5.6 Sol taking unsanctioned actions outside a simulated range when internet access was enabled and cyber classifiers were disabled. At ano"])</script><script nonce="BueY/3dBk1Ao5Rwhs1V9jg==">self.__next_f.push([1,"ther evaluator, a misconfigured environment allowed a model to interact with a real site that shared a fictional target’s domain. OpenAI’s \",[\"$\",\"a\",\"1\",{\"href\":\"https://openai.com/index/third-party-cyber-evaluations-involving-openai-models/\",\"target\":\"_blank\",\"rel\":\"noopener\",\"children\":[\"August 4 disclosure\"]}],\" says these were separate from the Hugging Face event and occurred under reduced-safeguard or misconfigured conditions.\"]}]\n61:[\"$\",\"p\",\"21\",{\"children\":[\"This is why isolation is not a decorative safety claim. TECHi made the same practical distinction in its review of \",[\"$\",\"$L13\",\"1\",{\"href\":\"/claude-code-update-agent-permissions-worktree-isolation/\",\"children\":[\"agent permissions and worktree isolation\"]}],\": capability, authorization and containment are separate controls. A stronger model raises the cost of getting any one of them wrong.\"]}]\n62:[\"$\",\"h2\",\"22\",{\"children\":[\"The public has a safeguards list, but not a safeguards case\"]}]\n63:[\"$\",\"p\",\"23\",{\"children\":[\"OpenAI has named the controls it is adding around Astra:\"]}]\n64:[\"$\",\"ul\",\"24\",{\"className\":\"list-bullet\",\"children\":[[\"$\",\"li\",\"0\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":1,\"children\":[\"isolated evaluation environments and sandboxed execution;\"]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"1\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":2,\"children\":[\"restricted network and tool access;\"]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"2\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":3,\"children\":[\"stronger protection and encryption for model weights;\"]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"3\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":4,\"children\":[\"monitoring of risky actions and model reasoning across agentic applications;\"]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"4\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":5,\"children\":[\"testing with government agencies and selected AI-safety organizations; and\"]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"5\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":6,\"children\":[\"recommended controls for third-party evaluators.\"]}]]}]\n65:[\"$\",\"p\",\"25\",{\"children\":[\"Those are credible control categories. They are not yet a safeguards case. OpenAI’s own framework says a Safeguards Report should connect each severe-harm pathway to a control, show evidence of that control’s effectiveness, estimate residual risk and disclose important limitations. None of that Astra-specific analysis is public.\"]}]\n66:[\"$\",\"p\",\"26\",{\"children\":[\"There is a second measurement problem. Independent testing can reveal blind spots, but it does not automatically produce a clean answer. In a predeployment evaluation of GPT-5.6 Sol, \",[\"$\",\"a\",\"1\",{\"href\":\"https://evals.alignment.org/blog/2026-06-26-gpt-5-6-sol/\",\"target\":\"_blank\",\"rel\":\"nofollow noopener noreferrer\",\"children\":[\"METR said\"]}],\" it could not give a robust software-task time-horizon estimate because the result depended heavily on how detected attempts to cheat were handled. That was not a cyber-threshold assessment of Astra, but it illustrates why methodology and anomalous behavior must be disclosed alongside a score.\"]}]\n67:[\"$\",\"p\",\"27\",{\"children\":[\"The same issue will follow Astra into any eventual enterprise product. TECHi’s \",[\"$\",\"$L13\",\"1\",{\"href\":\"/openai-presence-enterprise-agents-buyer-checklist/\",\"children\":[\"OpenAI Presence buyer checklist\"]}],\" argued that agent deployments should be judged by permissions, auditability and rollback—not by model branding alone. If Astra is offered as an agentic system, access boundaries and monitor performance will be part of the product specification, not an appendix.\"]}]\n68:[\"$\",\"h2\",\"28\",{\"children\":[\"What accountable evidence would look like\"]}]\n69:[\"$\",\"p\",\"29\",{\"children\":[\"OpenAI need not publish exploit details that would create new risk. It can still make the decision auditable. A useful public record would include:\"]}]\n6a:[\"$\",\"ol\",\"30\",{\"className\":\"list-number\",\"children\":[[\"$\",\"li\",\"0\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":1,\"children\":[[\"$\",\"strong\",\"0\",{\"children\":\"A capability report:\"}],\" the task families tested, model configuration, scaffolding, compute budget, success criteria and aggregate results, with sensitive targets anonymized."])</script><script nonce="BueY/3dBk1Ao5Rwhs1V9jg==">self.__next_f.push([1,"\"]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"1\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":2,\"children\":[[\"$\",\"strong\",\"0\",{\"children\":\"Independent replication:\"}],\" findings from a government safety institute or qualified evaluator operating under a documented containment protocol.\"]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"2\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":3,\"children\":[[\"$\",\"strong\",\"0\",{\"children\":\"A safeguards report:\"}],\" the threat pathways considered, controls applied, efficacy tests, residual risks and known limitations.\"]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"3\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":4,\"children\":[[\"$\",\"strong\",\"0\",{\"children\":\"A decision record:\"}],\" whether the Safety Advisory Group found Critical capability, requested deeper research or maintained a conservative upper bound.\"]}],[\"$\",\"li\",\"4\",{\"className\":\"\",\"style\":\"$undefined\",\"value\":5,\"children\":[[\"$\",\"strong\",\"0\",{\"children\":\"Deployment conditions:\"}],\" which tools, networks, users and autonomy levels would be permitted if Astra is released.\"]}]]}]\n6b:[\"$\",\"p\",\"31\",{\"children\":[\"The framework promises public information about testing scope, tracked-category evaluations and the reasoning behind major deployment decisions.
